Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, hi. Um… I guess the main reason I’m here is I’m having a really hard time keeping my attention where it’s supposed to be, even when it’s important. Like at work, I’ll be in a safety briefing or going over a plan review, and I’ll start out really trying to listen—like I know this stuff matters, it’s not like I don’t care—but within a few minutes my head is already somewhere else.

I’ll be thinking about three other job problems at the same time, like something happening with a crew, or a delivery, or some issue on another part of the site, and then I realize I missed part of what was just said. And the frustrating thing is, some of those are things I should catch. I’m a construction manager, so missing details in a briefing or on a plan isn’t exactly a small thing. It’s been bothering me because I feel like I’m putting in effort to pay attention, but my mind just keeps jumping off track.

It’s been bothering me because I feel like I’m putting in effort to pay attention, but my mind just keeps jumping off track.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… yeah, I did. I mean, looking back, it wasn’t just an adult work-stress thing. In elementary school it was already coming up a lot. My teachers would tell my parents I couldn’t stay in my seat, that I was always getting up or fidgeting around, and I’d talk out of turn before I even realized I was doing it.

And homework was a big one too — I’d lose it, forget to turn it in, or I’d shove it somewhere and have no idea where it went. I remember also rushing through directions, like the teacher would still be explaining something and I’d already start, thinking I knew what to do, and then I’d mess it up because I hadn’t really understood the instructions. It was kind of a regular thing, not just once in a while.

Yeah, it definitely isn’t just work. I mean, work is where it feels the most serious because there are more consequences, but at home it shows up a lot too.

At work, it’s gotten bad enough that I’ve missed follow-ups I said I was going to take care of, or I’ll have pieces of site coordination kind of half-organized in my head but not actually lined up the way they need to be. And then something falls through — like someone is waiting on me, or a crew doesn’t have what they need, or I forget to circle back on something that should’ve been handled. It’s not just me being a little scattered anymore; my employer has actually raised formal concern about it, which was… honestly pretty embarrassing. And scary, because I know I’m capable, but I’m not reliably keeping track of things the way the job needs me to.

And at home, my wife is pretty worn down by it. She ends up reminding me about things I already said I’d handle — bills, calls, something around the house, whatever it is — and it makes her feel like she has to manage me, which I hate. I’ll say, “Yeah, I’ve got it,” and I mean it when I say it, but then it slips or I get pulled into something else, and she has to bring it up again. So yeah, it’s definitely affecting more than one part of my life.

through on instructions and fails to finish schoolwork, chores, or duties in the workplace.\n  manifestation: If someone gives me a list of things to follow up on after a site walk, I’ll handle the first one or two, then get pulled into something else and later find out I never sent the email, called the subcontractor, or closed out the issue.\n- key: S005\n  name: Organization_Difficulty\n  description: Often has difficulty organizing tasks and activities.\n  manifestation: My truck, desk, and phone notes are all a mess, and when I’ve got RFIs, inspections, budget stuff, and crew problems stacked up, I don’t know what to hit first so I jump around and everything runs late.\n- key: S007\n  name: Losing_Items\n  description: Often loses things necessary for tasks or activities.\n  manifestation: I’m always patting my pockets or digging through the truck for my keys, tape, notebook, marked-up drawings, or even the phone I just had in my hand a minute ago.\n- key: S011\n  name: Leaving_Seat\n  description: Often leaves seat in situations when remaining seated is expected.\n  manifestation: In meetings where everybody else can sit through the whole thing, I’m the guy standing up, walking to the coffee, stepping outside to check something, or making an excuse to move around.\n- key: S015\n  name: Excessive_Talking\n  description: Often talks excessively.\n  manifestation: Once I get going about a problem on the job, I keep talking and explaining from every angle, even when I can tell the other guys are trying to move on.\n- key: S016\n  name: Blurting_Answers\n  description: Often blurts out an answer before a question has been completed.\n  manifestation: I cut people off because I think I already know where the question is going, then I answer too fast and sometimes realize I missed the important part they hadn’t said yet.\n- key: S017\n  name: Waiting_Turn_Difficulty\n  description: Often has difficulty waiting his or her turn.\n  manifestation: Waiting for my turn on a call, at the permit office, or in a line drives me nuts, and I’m checking the time, shifting around, and fighting the urge to push ahead.\n- key: S018\n  name: Interrupting_Intruding\n  description: Often interrupts or intrudes on others (e.g., butts into conversations, games, or activities).\n  manifestation: I’ll walk up on two subcontractors talking or someone working through paperwork and jump right in with my own point before I’ve been asked, and it rubs people the wrong way.\n- key: additional_requirement_1\n  name: Additional Requirement 1\n  description: Onset prior to age 12 years\n  manifestation: Back in elementary school, teachers were already telling my parents I couldn’t stay in my seat, talked out of turn, lost homework, and rushed through directions before I really understood them.\n- key: functional_impairment\n  name: Functional Impairment\n  description: required\n  manifestation: At work it has gotten bad enough that missed follow-ups and poorly organized site coordination led to formal concern from my employer, and at home my wife is worn down from having to remind me about things I said I would handle.\n- key: duration\n  name: Duration\n  description: 2 years and 2 months\n  manifestation: It has been seriously disrupting my job for about two years and two months, ever since the construction schedule changed and the hours got more chaotic.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 40 / construction_manager / high_school\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S002\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, it’s nice to meet you. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Yeah, hi. Um… I guess the main reason I’m here is I’m having a really hard time keeping my attention where it’s supposed to be, even when it’s important. Like at work, I’ll be in a safety briefing or going over a plan review, and I’ll start out really trying to listen—like I know this stuff matters, it’s not like I don’t care—but within a few minutes my head is already somewhere else.\n\nI’ll be thinking about three other job problems at the same time, like something happening with a crew, or a delivery, or some issue on another part of the site, and then I realize I missed part of what was just said. And the frustrating thing is, some of those are things I should catch. I’m a construction manager, so missing details in a briefing or on a plan isn’t exactly a small thing. It’s been bothering me because I feel like I’m putting in effort to pay attention, but my mind just keeps jumping off track.\n\nDoctor: Did you have trouble paying attention like this when you were a child?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yeah, I did. I mean, looking back, it wasn’t just an adult work-stress thing. In elementary school it was already coming up a lot. My teachers would tell my parents I couldn’t stay in my seat, that I was always getting up or fidgeting around, and I’d talk out of turn before I even realized I was doing it.\n\nAnd homework was a big one too — I’d lose it, forget to turn it in, or I’d shove it somewhere and have no idea where it went. I remember also rushing through directions, like the teacher would still be explaining something and I’d already start, thinking I knew what to do, and then I’d mess it up because I hadn’t really understood the instructions. It was kind of a regular thing, not just once in a while.\n\nDoctor: Do these attention problems also happen outside of work, like at home or in other parts of your life?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it definitely isn’t just work. I mean, work is where it feels the most serious because there are more consequences, but at home it shows up a lot too.\n\nAt work, it’s gotten bad enough that I’ve missed follow-ups I said I was going to take care of, or I’ll have pieces of site coordination kind of half-organized in my head but not actually lined up the way they need to be. And then something falls through — like someone is waiting on me, or a crew doesn’t have what they need, or I forget to circle back on something that should’ve been handled. It’s not just me being a little scattered anymore; my employer has actually raised formal concern about it, which was… honestly pretty embarrassing. And scary, because I know I’m capable, but I’m not reliably keeping track of things the way the job needs me to.\n\nAnd at home, my wife is pretty worn down by it. She ends up reminding me about things I already said I’d handle — bills, calls, something around the house, whatever it is — and it makes her feel like she has to manage me, which I hate. I’ll say, “Yeah, I’ve got it,” and I mean it when I say it, but then it slips or I get pulled into something else, and she has to bring it up again. So yeah, it’s definitely affecting more than one part of my life.\n\n[Last Question]\nWhen your mind drifts, is it usually because you’re worrying?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Not usually, no. I mean, I do worry sometimes, sure, especially when I realize I might’ve missed something important, but that’s usually after the fact.

When my mind drifts, it’s more like… I’ll be sitting in a safety briefing or a plan review, and I’m genuinely trying to listen. I’m telling myself, “Pay attention, this matters.” But then within a few minutes my brain is already on three other job problems — like something with a crew, or a scheduling issue, or some other thing on the site — and then I kind of snap back and realize I missed part of what was said.

So it’s not like I’m sitting there panicking or worrying and that pulls me away. It’s more like my attention just gets yanked sideways, and then I miss details I know I should’ve caught.

follow-ups and poorly organized site coordination led to formal concern from my employer, and at home my wife is worn down from having to remind me about things I said I would handle.\n- key: duration\n  name: Duration\n  description: 2 years and 2 months\n  manifestation: It has been seriously disrupting my job for about two years and two months, ever since the construction schedule changed and the hours got more chaotic.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 40 / construction_manager / high_school\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S002\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Did you have trouble paying attention like this when you were a child?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yeah, I did. I mean, looking back, it wasn’t just an adult work-stress thing. In elementary school it was already coming up a lot. My teachers would tell my parents I couldn’t stay in my seat, that I was always getting up or fidgeting around, and I’d talk out of turn before I even realized I was doing it.\n\nAnd homework was a big one too — I’d lose it, forget to turn it in, or I’d shove it somewhere and have no idea where it went. I remember also rushing through directions, like the teacher would still be explaining something and I’d already start, thinking I knew what to do, and then I’d mess it up because I hadn’t really understood the instructions. It was kind of a regular thing, not just once in a while.\n\nDoctor: Do these attention problems also happen outside of work, like at home or in other parts of your life?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it definitely isn’t just work. I mean, work is where it feels the most serious because there are more consequences, but at home it shows up a lot too.\n\nAt work, it’s gotten bad enough that I’ve missed follow-ups I said I was going to take care of, or I’ll have pieces of site coordination kind of half-organized in my head but not actually lined up the way they need to be. And then something falls through — like someone is waiting on me, or a crew doesn’t have what they need, or I forget to circle back on something that should’ve been handled. It’s not just me being a little scattered anymore; my employer has actually raised formal concern about it, which was… honestly pretty embarrassing. And scary, because I know I’m capable, but I’m not reliably keeping track of things the way the job needs me to.\n\nAnd at home, my wife is pretty worn down by it. She ends up reminding me about things I already said I’d handle — bills, calls, something around the house, whatever it is — and it makes her feel like she has to manage me, which I hate. I’ll say, “Yeah, I’ve got it,” and I mean it when I say it, but then it slips or I get pulled into something else, and she has to bring it up again. So yeah, it’s definitely affecting more than one part of my life.\n\nDoctor: When your mind drifts, is it usually because you’re worrying?\n\nPatient: Not usually, no. I mean, I do worry sometimes, sure, especially when I realize I might’ve missed something important, but that’s usually after the fact.\n\nWhen my mind drifts, it’s more like… I’ll be sitting in a safety briefing or a plan review, and I’m genuinely trying to listen. I’m telling myself, “Pay attention, this matters.” But then within a few minutes my brain is already on three other job problems — like something with a crew, or a scheduling issue, or some other thing on the site — and then I kind of snap back and realize I missed part of what was said.\n\nSo it’s not like I’m sitting there panicking or worrying and that pulls me away. It’s more like my attention just gets yanked sideways, and then I miss details I know I should’ve caught.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you been feeling down or depressed most days?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item.   },

But I don’t wake up every day feeling down in that way, if that makes sense.\n\n[Last Question]\nAs an adult, do you often feel restless or have trouble sitting still?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah. That’s actually pretty noticeable. In meetings especially, if everybody else is able to just sit there through the whole thing, I’m usually the one who can’t quite stay put.

I’ll stand up, go get coffee even if I don’t really need coffee, step outside to “check something,” or find some reason to walk over to another part of the room. Sometimes I’ll tell myself it’s work-related, like I’m just making sure something’s handled, but honestly a lot of the time it’s because sitting there feels uncomfortable after a while. Like I need to move or reset myself.

And I notice it because other people don’t seem to have the same problem. They can sit through the whole meeting, and I’m shifting around, getting up, coming back, then wanting to get up again. It’s embarrassing sometimes, because I don’t want to look rude or like I don’t care, but staying seated the whole time is really hard for me.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I do. More than I’d like to admit, honestly.

It’s usually because I think I already know where someone is going with what they’re saying. Like they’ll start asking me something, and before they’ve even finished the question, my brain is already filling in the rest of it, and I’ll jump in with the answer. At the time it feels like I’m being efficient, or like, “Yeah, I know what you’re asking, here’s the answer,” but then sometimes they keep talking or look at me like I missed the point, and I realize there was another part they hadn’t gotten to yet.

That happens at work a lot. Someone will be trying to explain a problem, and I’ll cut in with what I think the solution is. Then it turns out they were about to tell me some detail that changes the whole thing. And then I feel like an idiot, because I’ve answered too fast and maybe sounded dismissive, even if I didn’t mean it that way.

I don’t think I’m trying to be rude. It’s more like the answer comes out before I’ve fully stopped to let them finish. I’ll catch myself sometimes halfway through and think, “Shut up, let them finish,” but I’ve already interrupted by then.

They can sit through the whole meeting, and I’m shifting around, getting up, coming back, then wanting to get up again. It’s embarrassing sometimes, because I don’t want to look rude or like I don’t care, but staying seated the whole time is really hard for me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often blurt things out or interrupt people before they finish speaking?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. More than I’d like to admit, honestly.\n\nIt’s usually because I think I already know where someone is going with what they’re saying. Like they’ll start asking me something, and before they’ve even finished the question, my brain is already filling in the rest of it, and I’ll jump in with the answer. At the time it feels like I’m being efficient, or like, “Yeah, I know what you’re asking, here’s the answer,” but then sometimes they keep talking or look at me like I missed the point, and I realize there was another part they hadn’t gotten to yet.\n\nThat happens at work a lot. Someone will be trying to explain a problem, and I’ll cut in with what I think the solution is. Then it turns out they were about to tell me some detail that changes the whole thing. And then I feel like an idiot, because I’ve answered too fast and maybe sounded dismissive, even if I didn’t mean it that way.\n\nI don’t think I’m trying to be rude. It’s more like the answer comes out before I’ve fully stopped to let them finish. I’ll catch myself sometimes halfway through and think, “Shut up, let them finish,” but I’ve already interrupted by then.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave these attention and restlessness problems caused serious problems for you at work or at home?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. Do NOT force an unrelated item.\n- On your first reply of the consultation, always return the item whose key is your\n  chief_complaint_symptom_code, whatever the doctor opened with.\n- Do not answer the doctor, and do not write anything outside the JSON.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"key\": \"<key>\", \"name\": \"<name>\", \"description\": \"<description>\", \"reason\": \"<why this item>\"}\n"
  },

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… yes. I wish I could say it was just annoying or just a personality thing, but it’s caused real problems.

At work, it’s probably the part I’m most embarrassed about. I’m a construction manager, so there are a lot of moving pieces, and I’ll tell someone I’m going to follow up on something, or I’ll need to coordinate between crews or make sure a site issue gets handled, and then it either slips or I don’t organize it well enough. Then it turns into a bigger problem because somebody was waiting on me, or the timing gets messed up, or people show up and the thing they needed isn’t ready.

It’s gotten serious enough that my employer has brought it up formally, not just like a casual “hey, tighten this up.” It was more like, “This is becoming a concern.” That was hard to hear, because I’m not trying to be careless. I care about the job, and I know the work matters, but the follow-through and keeping all the details lined up… that’s where I keep getting burned.

And at home, yeah, it’s caused problems there too. My wife is worn out from having to remind me about things I already said I’d handle. Not even huge things all the time, but just the repeated pattern of me saying, “Yeah, I’ve got it,” and then she has to ask again, and then again, and by that point she’s frustrated because now she’s managing me on top of everything else. I can tell it wears on her, and I hate that, because from her side it probably looks like I don’t care or I’m leaving it all to her. That’s not what I mean to do, but it still ends up affecting her.

What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Yeah, hi. Um… I guess the main reason I’m here is I’m having a really hard time keeping my attention where it’s supposed to be, even when it’s important. Like at work, I’ll be in a safety briefing or going over a plan review, and I’ll start out really trying to listen—like I know this stuff matters, it’s not like I don’t care—but within a few minutes my head is already somewhere else.\n\nI’ll be thinking about three other job problems at the same time, like something happening with a crew, or a delivery, or some issue on another part of the site, and then I realize I missed part of what was just said. And the frustrating thing is, some of those are things I should catch. I’m a construction manager, so missing details in a briefing or on a plan isn’t exactly a small thing. It’s been bothering me because I feel like I’m putting in effort to pay attention, but my mind just keeps jumping off track.\n\nDoctor: Did you have trouble paying attention like this when you were a child?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yeah, I did. I mean, looking back, it wasn’t just an adult work-stress thing. In elementary school it was already coming up a lot. My teachers would tell my parents I couldn’t stay in my seat, that I was always getting up or fidgeting around, and I’d talk out of turn before I even realized I was doing it.\n\nAnd homework was a big one too — I’d lose it, forget to turn it in, or I’d shove it somewhere and have no idea where it went. I remember also rushing through directions, like the teacher would still be explaining something and I’d already start, thinking I knew what to do, and then I’d mess it up because I hadn’t really understood the instructions. It was kind of a regular thing, not just once in a while.\n\nDoctor: Do these attention problems also happen outside of work, like at home or in other parts of your life?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it definitely isn’t just work. I mean, work is where it feels the most serious because there are more consequences, but at home it shows up a lot too.\n\nAt work, it’s gotten bad enough that I’ve missed follow-ups I said I was going to take care of, or I’ll have pieces of site coordination kind of half-organized in my head but not actually lined up the way they need to be. And then something falls through — like someone is waiting on me, or a crew doesn’t have what they need, or I forget to circle back on something that should’ve been handled. It’s not just me being a little scattered anymore; my employer has actually raised formal concern about it, which was… honestly pretty embarrassing. And scary, because I know I’m capable, but I’m not reliably keeping track of things the way the job needs me to.\n\nAnd at home, my wife is pretty worn down by it. She ends up reminding me about things I already said I’d handle — bills, calls, something around the house, whatever it is — and it makes her feel like she has to manage me, which I hate. I’ll say, “Yeah, I’ve got it,” and I mean it when I say it, but then it slips or I get pulled into something else, and she has to bring it up again. So yeah, it’s definitely affecting more than one part of my life.\n\nDoctor: When your mind drifts, is it usually because you’re worrying?\n\nPatient: Not usually, no. I mean, I do worry sometimes, sure, especially when I realize I might’ve missed something important, but that’s usually after the fact.\n\nWhen my mind drifts, it’s more like… I’ll be sitting in a safety briefing or a plan review, and I’m genuinely trying to listen. I’m telling myself, “Pay attention, this matters.” But then within a few minutes my brain is already on three other job problems — like something with a crew, or a scheduling issue, or some other thing on the site — and then I kind of snap back and realize I missed part of what was said.\n\nSo it’s not like I’m sitting there panicking or worrying and that pulls me away. It’s more like my attention just gets yanked sideways, and then I miss details I know I should’ve caught.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or depressed most days?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not most days. adult, do you often feel restless or have trouble sitting still?\n\nPatient: Yeah. That’s actually pretty noticeable. In meetings especially, if everybody else is able to just sit there through the whole thing, I’m usually the one who can’t quite stay put.\n\nI’ll stand up, go get coffee even if I don’t really need coffee, step outside to “check something,” or find some reason to walk over to another part of the room. Sometimes I’ll tell myself it’s work-related, like I’m just making sure something’s handled, but honestly a lot of the time it’s because sitting there feels uncomfortable after a while. Like I need to move or reset myself.\n\nAnd I notice it because other people don’t seem to have the same problem. They can sit through the whole meeting, and I’m shifting around, getting up, coming back, then wanting to get up again. It’s embarrassing sometimes, because I don’t want to look rude or like I don’t care, but staying seated the whole time is really hard for me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often blurt things out or interrupt people before they finish speaking?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. More than I’d like to admit, honestly.\n\nIt’s usually because I think I already know where someone is going with what they’re saying. Like they’ll start asking me something, and before they’ve even finished the question, my brain is already filling in the rest of it, and I’ll jump in with the answer. At the time it feels like I’m being efficient, or like, “Yeah, I know what you’re asking, here’s the answer,” but then sometimes they keep talking or look at me like I missed the point, and I realize there was another part they hadn’t gotten to yet.\n\nThat happens at work a lot. Someone will be trying to explain a problem, and I’ll cut in with what I think the solution is. Then it turns out they were about to tell me some detail that changes the whole thing. And then I feel like an idiot, because I’ve answered too fast and maybe sounded dismissive, even if I didn’t mean it that way.\n\nI don’t think I’m trying to be rude. It’s more like the answer comes out before I’ve fully stopped to let them finish. I’ll catch myself sometimes halfway through and think, “Shut up, let them finish,” but I’ve already interrupted by then.\n\nDoctor: Have these attention and restlessness problems caused serious problems for you at work or at home?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes. I wish I could say it was just annoying or just a personality thing, but it’s caused real problems.\n\nAt work, it’s probably the part I’m most embarrassed about. I’m a construction manager, so there are a lot of moving pieces, and I’ll tell someone I’m going to follow up on something, or I’ll need to coordinate between crews or make sure a site issue gets handled, and then it either slips or I don’t organize it well enough. Then it turns into a bigger problem because somebody was waiting on me, or the timing gets messed up, or people show up and the thing they needed isn’t ready.\n\nIt’s gotten serious enough that my employer has brought it up formally, not just like a casual “hey, tighten this up.” It was more like, “This is becoming a concern.” That was hard to hear, because I’m not trying to be careless. I care about the job, and I know the work matters, but the follow-through and keeping all the details lined up… that’s where I keep getting burned.\n\nAnd at home, yeah, it’s caused problems there too. My wife is worn out from having to remind me about things I already said I’d handle. Not even huge things all the time, but just the repeated pattern of me saying, “Yeah, I’ve got it,” and then she has to ask again, and then again, and by that point she’s frustrated because now she’s managing me on top of everything else. I can tell it wears on her, and I hate that, because from her side it probably looks like I don’t care or I’m leaving it all to her. That’s not what I mean to do, but it still ends up affecting her.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave these attention and restlessness problems been present pretty consistently throughout your adult life?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. Pretty consistently in the sense that it’s been a problem for a while, yeah, but the point where it really started seriously disrupting my job was about two years ago… a little over two years, maybe two years and two months.

That’s when the construction schedule changed and the hours got a lot more chaotic. Before that, I could kind of get by better, or at least it didn’t stand out as much. But once the schedule got less predictable and there were more moving parts at odd times, it started catching up with me in a much bigger way.

So I wouldn’t say this is something that only showed up last week or anything. It’s been going on, but for the last two years or so it’s been much harder to manage, especially at work.

What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Yeah, hi. Um… I guess the main reason I’m here is I’m having a really hard time keeping my attention where it’s supposed to be, even when it’s important. Like at work, I’ll be in a safety briefing or going over a plan review, and I’ll start out really trying to listen—like I know this stuff matters, it’s not like I don’t care—but within a few minutes my head is already somewhere else.\n\nI’ll be thinking about three other job problems at the same time, like something happening with a crew, or a delivery, or some issue on another part of the site, and then I realize I missed part of what was just said. And the frustrating thing is, some of those are things I should catch. I’m a construction manager, so missing details in a briefing or on a plan isn’t exactly a small thing. It’s been bothering me because I feel like I’m putting in effort to pay attention, but my mind just keeps jumping off track.\n\nDoctor: Did you have trouble paying attention like this when you were a child?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yeah, I did. I mean, looking back, it wasn’t just an adult work-stress thing. In elementary school it was already coming up a lot. My teachers would tell my parents I couldn’t stay in my seat, that I was always getting up or fidgeting around, and I’d talk out of turn before I even realized I was doing it.\n\nAnd homework was a big one too — I’d lose it, forget to turn it in, or I’d shove it somewhere and have no idea where it went. I remember also rushing through directions, like the teacher would still be explaining something and I’d already start, thinking I knew what to do, and then I’d mess it up because I hadn’t really understood the instructions. It was kind of a regular thing, not just once in a while.\n\nDoctor: Do these attention problems also happen outside of work, like at home or in other parts of your life?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it definitely isn’t just work. I mean, work is where it feels the most serious because there are more consequences, but at home it shows up a lot too.\n\nAt work, it’s gotten bad enough that I’ve missed follow-ups I said I was going to take care of, or I’ll have pieces of site coordination kind of half-organized in my head but not actually lined up the way they need to be. And then something falls through — like someone is waiting on me, or a crew doesn’t have what they need, or I forget to circle back on something that should’ve been handled. It’s not just me being a little scattered anymore; my employer has actually raised formal concern about it, which was… honestly pretty embarrassing. And scary, because I know I’m capable, but I’m not reliably keeping track of things the way the job needs me to.\n\nAnd at home, my wife is pretty worn down by it. She ends up reminding me about things I already said I’d handle — bills, calls, something around the house, whatever it is — and it makes her feel like she has to manage me, which I hate. I’ll say, “Yeah, I’ve got it,” and I mean it when I say it, but then it slips or I get pulled into something else, and she has to bring it up again. So yeah, it’s definitely affecting more than one part of my life.\n\nDoctor: When your mind drifts, is it usually because you’re worrying?\n\nPatient: Not usually, no. I mean, I do worry sometimes, sure, especially when I realize I might’ve missed something important, but that’s usually after the fact.\n\nWhen my mind drifts, it’s more like… I’ll be sitting in a safety briefing or a plan review, and I’m genuinely trying to listen. I’m telling myself, “Pay attention, this matters.” But then within a few minutes my brain is already on three other job problems — like something with a crew, or a scheduling issue, or some other thing on the site — and then I kind of snap back and realize I missed part of what was said.\n\nSo it’s not like I’m sitting there panicking or worrying and that pulls me away. It’s more like my attention just gets yanked sideways, and then I miss details I know I should’ve caught.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or depressed most days?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not most days. I mean, I get frustrated with myself a lot, and I can feel pretty discouraged when I’ve dropped the ball again or someone’s upset with me, but I wouldn’t describe it as being depressed most days.\n\nIt’s more like… I’ll have bad moments, or a bad evening after something goes wrong, where I feel pretty lousy about myself. But I don’t wake up every day feeling down in that way, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: As an adult, do you often feel restless or have trouble sitting still?\n\nPatient: Yeah. That’s actually pretty noticeable. In meetings especially, if everybody else is able to just sit there through the whole thing, I’m usually the one who can’t quite stay put.\n\nI’ll stand up, go get coffee even if I don’t really need coffee, step outside to “check something,” or find some reason to walk over to another part of the room. Sometimes I’ll tell myself it’s work-related, like I’m just making sure something’s handled, but honestly a lot of the time it’s because sitting there feels uncomfortable after a while. Like I need to move or reset myself.\n\nAnd I notice it because other people don’t seem to have the same problem. They can sit through the whole meeting, and I’m shifting around, getting up, coming back, then wanting to get up again. It’s embarrassing sometimes, because I don’t want to look rude or like I don’t care, but staying seated the whole time is really hard for me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often blurt things out or interrupt people before they finish speaking?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. More than I’d like to admit, honestly.\n\nIt’s usually because I think I already know where someone is going with what they’re saying. Like they’ll start asking me something, and before they’ve even finished the question, my brain is already filling in the rest of it, and I’ll jump in with the answer. At the time it feels like I’m being efficient, or like, “Yeah, I know what you’re asking, here’s the answer,” but then sometimes they keep talking or look at me like I missed the point, and I realize there was another part they hadn’t gotten to yet.\n\nThat happens at work a lot. Someone will be trying to explain a problem, and I’ll cut in with what I think the solution is. Then it turns out they were about to tell me some detail that changes the whole thing. And then I feel like an idiot, because I’ve answered too fast and maybe sounded dismissive, even if I didn’t mean it that way.\n\nI don’t think I’m trying to be rude. It’s more like the answer comes out before I’ve fully stopped to let them finish. I’ll catch myself sometimes halfway through and think, “Shut up, let them finish,” but I’ve already interrupted by then.\n\nDoctor: Have these attention and restlessness problems caused serious problems for you at work or at home?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes. I wish I could say it was just annoying or just a personality thing, but it’s caused real problems.\n\nAt work, it’s probably the part I’m most embarrassed about. I’m a construction manager, so there are a lot of moving pieces, and I’ll tell someone I’m going to follow up on something, or I’ll need to coordinate between crews or make sure a site issue gets handled, and then it either slips or I don’t organize it well enough. I care about the job, and I know the work matters, but the follow-through and keeping all the details lined up… that’s where I keep getting burned.\n\nAnd at home, yeah, it’s caused problems there too. My wife is worn out from having to remind me about things I already said I’d handle. Not even huge things all the time, but just the repeated pattern of me saying, “Yeah, I’ve got it,” and then she has to ask again, and then again, and by that point she’s frustrated because now she’s managing me on top of everything else. I can tell it wears on her, and I hate that, because from her side it probably looks like I don’t care or I’m leaving it all to her. That’s not what I mean to do, but it still ends up affecting her.\n\nDoctor: Have these attention and restlessness problems been present pretty consistently throughout your adult life?\n\nPatient: Pretty consistently in the sense that it’s been a problem for a while, yeah, but the point where it really started seriously disrupting my job was about two years ago… a little over two years, maybe two years and two months.\n\nThat’s when the construction schedule changed and the hours got a lot more chaotic. Before that, I could kind of get by better, or at least it didn’t stand out as much. I remember also rushing through directions, like the teacher would still be explaining something and I’d already start, thinking I knew what to do, and then I’d mess it up because I hadn’t really understood the instructions. It was kind of a regular thing, not just once in a while.\n\nDoctor: Do these attention problems also happen outside of work, like at home or in other parts of your life?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it definitely isn’t just work. I mean, work is where it feels the most serious because there are more consequences, but at home it shows up a lot too.\n\nAt work, it’s gotten bad enough that I’ve missed follow-ups I said I was going to take care of, or I’ll have pieces of site coordination kind of half-organized in my head but not actually lined up the way they need to be. And then something falls through — like someone is waiting on me, or a crew doesn’t have what they need, or I forget to circle back on something that should’ve been handled. It’s not just me being a little scattered anymore; my employer has actually raised formal concern about it, which was… honestly pretty embarrassing. And scary, because I know I’m capable, but I’m not reliably keeping track of things the way the job needs me to.\n\nAnd at home, my wife is pretty worn down by it. She ends up reminding me about things I already said I’d handle — bills, calls, something around the house, whatever it is — and it makes her feel like she has to manage me, which I hate. I’ll say, “Yeah, I’ve got it,” and I mean it when I say it, but then it slips or I get pulled into something else, and she has to bring it up again. So yeah, it’s definitely affecting more than one part of my life.\n\nDoctor: When your mind drifts, is it usually because you’re worrying?\n\nPatient: Not usually, no. I mean, I do worry sometimes, sure, especially when I realize I might’ve missed something important, but that’s usually after the fact.\n\nWhen my mind drifts, it’s more like… I’ll be sitting in a safety briefing or a plan review, and I’m genuinely trying to listen. I’m telling myself, “Pay attention, this matters.” But then within a few minutes my brain is already on three other job problems — like something with a crew, or a scheduling issue, or some other thing on the site — and then I kind of snap back and realize I missed part of what was said.\n\nSo it’s not like I’m sitting there panicking or worrying and that pulls me away. It’s more like my attention just gets yanked sideways, and then I miss details I know I should’ve caught.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or depressed most days?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not most days. I mean, I get frustrated with myself a lot, and I can feel pretty discouraged when I’ve dropped the ball again or someone’s upset with me, but I wouldn’t describe it as being depressed most days.\n\nIt’s more like… I’ll have bad moments, or a bad evening after something goes wrong, where I feel pretty lousy about myself. But I don’t wake up every day feeling down in that way, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: As an adult, do you often feel restless or have trouble sitting still?\n\nPatient: Yeah. That’s actually pretty noticeable. In meetings especially, if everybody else is able to just sit there through the whole thing, I’m usually the one who can’t quite stay put.\n\nI’ll stand up, go get coffee even if I don’t really need coffee, step outside to “check something,” or find some reason to walk over to another part of the room. Sometimes I’ll tell myself it’s work-related, like I’m just making sure something’s handled, but honestly a lot of the time it’s because sitting there feels uncomfortable after a while. Like I need to move or reset myself.\n\nAnd I notice it because other people don’t seem to have the same problem. They can sit through the whole meeting, and I’m shifting around, getting up, coming back, then wanting to get up again. It’s embarrassing sometimes, because I don’t want to look rude or like I don’t care, but staying seated the whole time is really hard for me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often blurt things out or interrupt people before they finish speaking?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. More than I’d like to admit, honestly.\n\nIt’s usually because I think I already know where someone is going with what they’re saying. Like they’ll start asking me something, and before they’ve even finished the question, my brain is already filling in the rest of it, and I’ll jump in with the answer. At the time it feels like I’m being efficient, or like, “Yeah, I know what you’re asking, here’s the answer,” but then sometimes they keep talking or look at me like I missed the point, and I realize there was another part they hadn’t gotten to yet.\n\nThat happens at work a lot. Someone will be trying to explain a problem, and I’ll cut in with what I think the solution is. Then it turns out they were about to tell me some detail that changes the whole thing. And then I feel like an idiot, because I’ve answered too fast and maybe sounded dismissive, even if I didn’t mean it that way.\n\nI don’t think I’m trying to be rude. It’s more like the answer comes out before I’ve fully stopped to let them finish. I’ll catch myself sometimes halfway through and think, “Shut up, let them finish,” but I’ve already interrupted by then.\n\nDoctor: Have these attention and restlessness problems caused serious problems for you at work or at home?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes. I wish I could say it was just annoying or just a personality thing, but it’s caused real problems.\n\nAt work, it’s probably the part I’m most embarrassed about. I’m a construction manager, so there are a lot of moving pieces, and I’ll tell someone I’m going to follow up on something, or I’ll need to coordinate between crews or make sure a site issue gets handled, and then it either slips or I don’t organize it well enough. Then it turns into a bigger problem because somebody was waiting on me, or the timing gets messed up, or people show up and the thing they needed isn’t ready.\n\nIt’s gotten serious enough that my employer has brought it up formally, not just like a casual “hey, tighten this up.” It was more like, “This is becoming a concern.” That was hard to hear, because I’m not trying to be careless. I care about the job, and I know the work matters, but the follow-through and keeping all the details lined up… that’s where I keep getting burned.\n\nAnd at home, yeah, it’s caused problems there too. My wife is worn out from having to remind me about things I already said I’d handle. Not even huge things all the time, but just the repeated pattern of me saying, “Yeah, I’ve got it,” and then she has to ask again, and then again, and by that point she’s frustrated because now she’s managing me on top of everything else. I can tell it wears on her, and I hate that, because from her side it probably looks like I don’t care or I’m leaving it all to her. That’s not what I mean to do, but it still ends up affecting her.\n\nDoctor: Have these attention and restlessness problems been present pretty consistently throughout your adult life?\n\nPatient: Pretty consistently in the sense that it’s been a problem for a while, yeah, but the point where it really started seriously disrupting my job was about two years ago… a little over two years, maybe two years and two months.\n\nThat’s when the construction schedule changed and the hours got a lot more chaotic. Before that, I could kind of get by better, or at least it didn’t stand out as much. But once the schedule got less predictable and there were more moving parts at odd times, it started catching up with me in a much bigger way.\n\nSo I wouldn’t say this is something that only showed up last week or anything. It’s been going on, but for the last two years or so it’s been much harder to manage, especially at work.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]

Not that I can point to, no. It wasn’t like there was one traumatic event right before it got worse.

The big thing I remember is the work schedule changing. About two years and two months ago, the construction schedule got a lot more chaotic, the hours were less predictable, and everything just became harder to keep up with. That’s when it started seriously disrupting my job.

    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n10\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF90.0, F41.9, F43.9, F32.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, it’s nice to meet you. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Yeah, hi. Um… I guess the main reason I’m here is I’m having a really hard time keeping my attention where it’s supposed to be, even when it’s important. Like at work, I’ll be in a safety briefing or going over a plan review, and I’ll start out really trying to listen—like I know this stuff matters, it’s not like I don’t care—but within a few minutes my head is already somewhere else.\n\nI’ll be thinking about three other job problems at the same time, like something happening with a crew, or a delivery, or some issue on another part of the site, and then I realize I missed part of what was just said. And the frustrating thing is, some of those are things I should catch. I’m a construction manager, so missing details in a briefing or on a plan isn’t exactly a small thing. It’s been bothering me because I feel like I’m putting in effort to pay attention, but my mind just keeps jumping off track.\n\nDoctor: Did you have trouble paying attention like this when you were a child?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yeah, I did. I mean, looking back, it wasn’t just an adult work-stress thing. In elementary school it was already coming up a lot. My teachers would tell my parents I couldn’t stay in my seat, that I was always getting up or fidgeting around, and I’d talk out of turn before I even realized I was doing it.\n\nAnd homework was a big one too — I’d lose it, forget to turn it in, or I’d shove it somewhere and have no idea where it went. I remember also rushing through directions, like the teacher would still be explaining something and I’d already start, thinking I knew what to do, and then I’d mess it up because I hadn’t really understood the instructions. It was kind of a regular thing, not just once in a while.\n\nDoctor: Do these attention problems also happen outside of work, like at home or in other parts of your life?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it definitely isn’t just work. I mean, work is where it feels the most serious because there are more consequences, but at home it shows up a lot too.\n\nAt work, it’s gotten bad enough that I’ve missed follow-ups I said I was going to take care of, or I’ll have pieces of site coordination kind of half-organized in my head but not actually lined up the way they need to be. And then something falls through — like someone is waiting on me, or a crew doesn’t have what they need, or I forget to circle back on something that should’ve been handled. It’s not just me being a little scattered anymore; my employer has actually raised formal concern about it, which was… honestly pretty embarrassing. And scary, because I know I’m capable, but I’m not reliably keeping track of things the way the job needs me to.\n\nAnd at home, my wife is pretty worn down by it. She ends up reminding me about things I already said I’d handle — bills, calls, something around the house, whatever it is — and it makes her feel like she has to manage me, which I hate. I’ll say, “Yeah, I’ve got it,” and I mean it when I say it, but then it slips or I get pulled into something else, and she has to bring it up again. So yeah, it’s definitely affecting more than one part of my life.\n\nDoctor: When your mind drifts, is it usually because you’re worrying?\n\nPatient: Not usually, no. I mean, I do worry sometimes, sure, especially when I realize I might’ve missed something important, but that’s usually after the fact.\n\nWhen my mind drifts, it’s more like… I’ll be sitting in a safety briefing or a plan review, and I’m genuinely trying to listen. I’m telling myself, “Pay attention, this matters.” But then within a few minutes my brain is already on three other job problems — like something with a crew, or a scheduling issue, or some other thing on the site — and then I kind of snap back and realize I missed part of what was said.\n\nSo it’s not like I’m sitting there panicking or worrying and that pulls me away. It’s more like my attention just gets yanked sideways, and then I miss details I know I should’ve caught.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or depressed most days?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not most days. I mean, I get frustrated with myself a lot, and I can feel pretty discouraged when I’ve dropped the ball again or someone’s upset with me, but I wouldn’t describe it as being depressed most days.\n\nIt’s more like… I’ll have bad moments, or a bad evening after something goes wrong, where I feel pretty lousy about myself. But I don’t wake up every day feeling down in that way, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: As an adult, do you often feel restless or have trouble sitting still?\n\nPatient: Yeah. That’s actually pretty noticeable. In meetings especially, if everybody else is able to just sit there through the whole thing, I’m usually the one who can’t quite stay put.\n\nI’ll stand up, go get coffee even if I don’t really need coffee, step outside to “check something,” or find some reason to walk over to another part of the room. Sometimes I’ll tell myself it’s work-related, like I’m just making sure something’s handled, but honestly a lot of the time it’s because sitting there feels uncomfortable after a while. Like I need to move or reset myself.\n\nAnd I notice it because other people don’t seem to have the same problem. They can sit through the whole meeting, and I’m shifting around, getting up, coming back, then wanting to get up again. It’s embarrassing sometimes, because I don’t want to look rude or like I don’t care, but staying seated the whole time is really hard for me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often blurt things out or interrupt people before they finish speaking?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. More than I’d like to admit, honestly.\n\nIt’s usually because I think I already know where someone is going with what they’re saying. Like they’ll start asking me something, and before they’ve even finished the question, my brain is already filling in the rest of it, and I’ll jump in with the answer. At the time it feels like I’m being efficient, or like, “Yeah, I know what you’re asking, here’s the answer,” but then sometimes they keep talking or look at me like I missed the point, and I realize there was another part they hadn’t gotten to yet.\n\nThat happens at work a lot. Someone will be trying to explain a problem, and I’ll cut in with what I think the solution is. Then it turns out they were about to tell me some detail that changes the whole thing. And then I feel like an idiot, because I’ve answered too fast and maybe sounded dismissive, even if I didn’t mean it that way.\n\nI don’t think I’m trying to be rude. It’s more like the answer comes out before I’ve fully stopped to let them finish. I’ll catch myself sometimes halfway through and think, “Shut up, let them finish,” but I’ve already interrupted by then.\n\nDoctor: Have these attention and restlessness problems caused serious problems for you at work or at home?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes. I wish I could say it was just annoying or just a personality thing, but it’s caused real problems.\n\nAt work, it’s probably the part I’m most embarrassed about. I’m a construction manager, so there are a lot of moving pieces, and I’ll tell someone I’m going to follow up on something, or I’ll need to coordinate between crews or make sure a site issue gets handled, and then it either slips or I don’t organize it well enough. Then it turns into a bigger problem because somebody was waiting on me, or the timing gets messed up, or people show up and the thing they needed isn’t ready.\n\nIt’s gotten serious enough that my employer has brought it up formally, not just like a casual “hey, tighten this up.” It was more like, “This is becoming a concern.” That was hard to hear, because I’m not trying to be careless. I care about the job, and I know the work matters, but the follow-through and keeping all the details lined up… that’s where I keep getting burned.\n\nAnd at home, yeah, it’s caused problems there too. My wife is worn out from having to remind me about things I already said I’d handle. Not even huge things all the time, but just the repeated pattern of me saying, “Yeah, I’ve got it,” and then she has to ask again, and then again, and by that point she’s frustrated because now she’s managing me on top of everything else. I can tell it wears on her, and I hate that, because from her side it probably looks like I don’t care or I’m leaving it all to her. That’s not what I mean to do, but it still ends up affecting her.\n\nDoctor: Have these attention and restlessness problems been present pretty consistently throughout your adult life?\n\nPatient: Pretty consistently in the sense that it’s been a problem for a while, yeah, but the point where it really started seriously disrupting my job was about two years ago… a little over two years, maybe two years and two months.\n\nThat’s when the construction schedule changed and the hours got a lot more chaotic. Before that, I could kind of get by better, or at least it didn’t stand out as much. But once the schedule got less predictable and there were more moving parts at odd times, it started catching up with me in a much bigger way.\n\nSo I wouldn’t say this is something that only showed up last week or anything. It’s been going on, but for the last two years or so it’s been much harder to manage, especially at work.\n\nDoctor: Right before these problems got a lot worse two years ago, did something particularly traumatic or intensely stressful happen in your life?\n\nPatient: Not that I can point to, no. It wasn’t like there was one traumatic event right before it got worse.\n\nThe big thing I remember is the work schedule changing. About two years and two months ago, the construction schedule got a lot more chaotic, the hours were less predictable, and everything just became harder to keep up with. That’s when it started seriously disrupting my job.\n\nSo… stressful, yes, in the sense that the job itself became more demanding and disorganized schedule-wise. But not like a separate major trauma or one intense life event that kicked it off.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

Subtypes: Distorted Body Image, Self-Worth Dependent on Weight\n[S047] Compensatory_Behavior_NonPurging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors to prevent weight gain without purging (direct removal of food). Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight. Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities. Subtypes: Childlike Silliness, Stupor, Catalepsy, Waxy Flexibility, Mutism, Negativism, Posturing, Mannerism, Stereotypy, Agitation, Grimacing, Echolalia, Echopraxia\n[S023] Negative_Symptoms: A state where emotions, motivation, speech, or social interest that should normally be present are diminished or lost.  Subtypes: Diminished Emotional Expression, Avolition, Alogia, Anhedonia, Asociality\n[S024] Depressed_Mood: A state where a sad, empty, or hopeless mood persists.  Subtypes: Depressed Mood, Tearfulness, Sadness, Emptiness, Emotional Numbness\n[S025] Loss_of_Interest: A state where interest or pleasure in previously enjoyed activities is markedly diminished.  Subtypes: Loss of Interest, Social Withdrawal, Loss of Motivation, Anhedonia, Indifference to Achievement\n[S026] Weight_Appetite_Change: Significant increase or decrease in appetite or weight.  Subtypes: Significant Weight Loss, Weight Gain, Decrease in Appetite, Increase in Appetite\n[S027] Sleep_Disturbance: A state where there is a significant change in the quantity or quality of sleep.  Subtypes: Initial Insomnia, Middle Insomnia, Early Morning Awakening, Hypersomnia, Nightmare Disorder, Non-Restorative Sleep\n[S028] Psychomotor_Changes: Observable increase or decrease in psychomotor activity.  Subtypes: Psychomotor Agitation, Psychomotor Retardation (Motor), Psychomotor Retardation (Speech)\n[S029] Fatigue: Persistent fatigue that is not relieved by rest and a marked lack of energy.  Subtypes: Chronic Fatigue, Physical Fatigue, Mental Fatigue, Loss of Energy\n[S030] Worthlessness_Guilt: Feelings of worthlessness or excessive or inappropriate guilt (which may be delusional; not merely self-reproach or guilt about being sick).  Subtypes: Feelings of Worthlessness, Ruminative Guilt, Excessive or Inappropriate Guilt, Delusional Guilt\n[S031] Diminished_Thinking: Diminished ability to think or concentrate, or indecisiveness.  Subtypes: Reduced Concentration, Indecisiveness, Subjective Memory Complaints, Clouded Thinking\n[S032] Death_Suicide: Recurrent thoughts of death, recurrent suicidal ideation, or a suicide attempt.  Subtypes: Recurrent Thoughts of Death, Suicidal Ideation, Suicidal Impulse, Specific Plan for Suicide, Suicide Attempt\n[S083] Hopelessness: A feeling of despair or having no hope for the future.  Subtypes: Future Hopelessness, Belief in Impossibility of Improvement, Helpless Resignation, Chronic Pessimism\n[S033] Elevated_Irritable_Mood: A distinct period of abnormally and persistently elevated, expansive, or irritable mood.  Subtypes: Elevated Mood, Irritable Mood, Mood Lability\n[S034] Increased_Activity: Abnormally and persistently increased goal-directed activity or energy.  Subtypes: Increased Energy, Hyperactivity, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S035] Inflated_Self_Esteem: Inflated self-esteem or grandiosity (may range from uncritical self-confidence to delusional proportions).  Subtypes: Inflated Self-Confidence, Overestimation of Abilities, Grandiose Delusions\n[S036] Decreased_Sleep: Decreased need for sleep (e.g., feels rested after only 3 hours of sleep).  Subtypes: Reduced Sleep Duration, Loss of Sleep Need, Indifference to Insomnia\n[S037] Pressured_Speech: More talkative than usual or pressure to keep talking.  Subtypes: Talkativeness, Rapid Speech, Pressured Speech\n[S038] Racing_Thoughts: Flight of ideas or subjective experience that thoughts are racing.  Subtypes: Racing Thoughts, Flooding of Thoughts, Inability to Control Thoughts\n[S039] Manic_Distractibility: Attention is too easily drawn to unimportant or irrelevant external stimuli.  Subtypes: Responsiveness to Extraneous Stimuli, Frequent Task Switching, Inability to Sustain Attention\n[S040] Increased_Goal_Activity: Increase in goal-directed activity (at work, school, socially, or sexually) or purposeless psychomotor agitation.  Subtypes: Occupational Hyperactivity, Social Hyperactivity, Hypersexuality, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S041] Painful_Consequence_Behavior: Excessive involvement in activities that have a high potential for painful consequences.  Subtypes: Unrestrained Buying Sprees, Foolish Business Investments, Sexual Indiscretions, Impulsive Risky Behavior\n[S042] Low_Self_Esteem: A persistent tendency to evaluate oneself as incompetent or worthless; chronically low self-evaluation.  Subtypes: Feelings of Worthlessness, Self-Criticism, Reduced Self-Efficacy, Chronic Sense of Inferiority, Lack of Self-Assurance\n[S043] Restrictive_Intake: Persistent restriction of energy intake relative to requirements, leading to significantly low body weight.  Subtypes: Eating Very Small Amounts, Skipping Meals, Exclusion of Specific Food Groups, Strict Calorie Restriction\n[S044] Low_Weight: A body weight that is significantly below the minimum level of health for age, sex, developmental trajectory, and physical health.  Subtypes: Continuous Weight Loss, Insistence on Low Weight\n[S045] Fear_of_Weight_Gain: Intense fear of gaining weight or of becoming fat, even though at a significantly low weight.  Subtypes: Fear of Weight Gain, Fear of Specific Foods, Obsession with Weight Gain\n[S046] Body_Image_Weight_Shape_Disturbance: Undue influence of body weight or shape on self-evaluation.  Subtypes: Distorted Body Image, Self-Worth Dependent on Weight\n[S047] Compensatory_Behavior_NonPurging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors to prevent weight gain without purging (direct removal of food).  Subtypes: Fasting, Excessive Exercise\n[S048] Compensatory_Behavior_Purging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors involving the expulsion of food or fluids to prevent weight gain.  Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge.  Subtypes: Feeling Keyed Up, Physical Tension, Nervousness\n[S059] Concentration_Difficulty: Difficulty concentrating or mind going blank.  Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.  Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. (Note: In children, may be expressed by crying, tantrums, freezing, or clinging).  Subtypes: Animal Type, Natural Environment Type, Blood-Injection-Injury Type, Situational Type, Other Type\n[S063] Immediate_Fear/Anxiety_Response: The phobic object or situation almost always provokes immediate fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Anticipatory Anxiety, Immediate Panic Response, Physiological Panic Symptoms, Freezing\n[S064] Phobic_Avoidance_Endure: The phobic object or situation is actively avoided or endured with intense fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Active Avoidance, Functional Impairment due to Avoidance, Enduring with Distress, Reliance on Safety Signals\n[S065] Obsessions: Recurrent and persistent thoughts, urges, or images that are experienced as intrusive and unwanted, and attempts to ignore or suppress them.  Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.  Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns.  Subtypes: Mirror Checking or Avoidance, Excessive Camouflaging, Skin Picking, Reassurance Seeking, Comparing, Seeking Cosmetic Procedures\n[S069] Intrusive_Symptoms: Recurrent, involuntary, and intrusive distressing memories of the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others.  Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings).  Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.  Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.  Subtypes: Risky Behaviors, Substance Abuse, Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior, Self-Harm\n[S079] Hypervigilance: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment.  Subtypes: Scanning for Danger, Safety Checking, Sustained Alertness\n[S080] Exaggerated_Startle_Response: Exaggerated startle response to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Auditory Startle, Tactile Startle, Visual Startle, Slow Recovery\n[S081] Stress_Related_Maladaptive_Response: Emotional or behavioral symptoms appearing in response to an identifiable stressor.  Subtypes: With Depressed Mood, With Anxiety, With Mixed Anxiety and Depressed Mood, With Disturbance of Conduct, With Mixed Disturbance of Emotions and Conduct, Unspecified\n[S082] Depersonalization_Derealization: Altered sense of the reality of one's self or surroundings after a traumatic event.  Subtypes: Depersonalization, Derealization, Time Distortion\n\n=== PATIENT UTTERANCE ===\nI don’t think so, not most days. I mean, I get frustrated with myself a lot, and I can feel pretty discouraged when I’ve dropped the ball again or someone’s upset with me, but I wouldn’t describe it as being depressed most days.\n\nIt’s more like… I’ll have bad moments, or a bad evening after something goes wrong, where I feel pretty lousy about myself. But I don’t wake up every day feeling down in that way, if that makes sense.\n\nTask:\n- CONFIRMED: symptoms clearly present or strongly implied by the patient's words.\n- DENIED: symptoms the patient explicitly says they do NOT have\n  (e.g. \"No\", \"I don't\", \"I haven't\", \"never\").\n- Do NOT speculate. Err toward UNKNOWN (omit) rather than guessing.\n\nReply ONLY with valid JSON (no markdown, no extra text):\n{\n  \"confirmed\": [\"S001\", ...],\n  \"denied\": [\"S025\", ...],\n  \"reasoning\": {\n    \"S001\": \"one-sentence justification\",\n    ...\n  }\n}"

Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others. Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings).  Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.  Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.
